--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page continues a technical discussion on the physics of heat and fire, specifically focusing on the properties of reflection and thermal expansion in various substances.</meta>

...in wood in bowls. For just as by this action, bodies heat up much more and therefore occupy more space—even if they should creep into a rough surface or rather disperse it—so too the particles of fire, as they enter closer into some body, and by that motion, it constricts [the particles].

7th: Concerning the reflection of fire from the **Sun** <note>The symbol ☉ in the original text represents the Sun.</note>, it can be reflected to a very great distance, as mirrors show; if one applies fire to the focus of one [mirror], ignition occurs in the focus of another even if it is quite far removed from the first.

8th: It is easily understood that the fire from the **Sun** can also strike against a surface and be reflected from it. From this? Whether by the effect of impenetrability, etc.

<margin>as in the 5th [point]</margin> 9th: That fire expands bodies is evident. Thus: Fire heats bodies and expands into them; fire also expands into a thing by the <term>Power</term> <note>original Latin: *Potentia*. In early modern physics, this refers to the active force or inherent capability of a substance—here, heat—to produce a physical effect.</note> of heat, whether that thing be fluid or solid. As experience teaches in the case of air, water, spirit of wine <note>High-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>, oil, and oily spirits, <term>Mercury</term> <note>original Latin: *Mercurio*; also known as quicksilver.</note>, and even in stones, iron, copper, silver, gold, and <unclear>others</unclear>, it was clear that they expand. Truly, that other fluids expand is demonstrated thus:

Take a long glass tube having a flask <note>original Latin: *ampulla*; a rounded glass bulb at the base of the tube.</note> at the bottom, also filled with fluid in such a way that it can be submerged in a warm spring of water; one will observe that immediately after immersion, the fluid inside descends a certain distance down into the tube, but then truly rises to a remarkable height. <note>This "descending then rising" is a classic scientific observation: the glass flask expands first, increasing its internal volume and making the liquid level drop, before the liquid itself heats up and expands further.</note> Water to which something has been mixed cannot show this for long, as experiments on heat teach. This demonstrates that just as with fluids, solids are expanded by heat; for when a fluid is immersed in warm water, or rather enclosed in a tube, [the heat] causes it to descend for a short while, then expansion occurs into that very thing because the <unclear>atoms</unclear> of heat rush in warmly and expand those <unclear>atoms</unclear> <note>The author uses the term "atoms" to describe the fundamental particles of both heat and the matter it acts upon.</note>—since from this explanation...

<summary>This page discusses the reflection of solar heat using mirrors and details experiments regarding the thermal expansion of solids and fluids. It describes a specific experiment with a glass bulb and tube to demonstrate how heat particles interact with and expand different materials.</summary>

Such transitions are common in "Sammelbände"—volumes where several distinct books or pamphlets were bound together by a later owner.</meta>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the "Treasury of Exorcisms" (1608). In early modern publishing, authors or publishers would dedicate their work to a powerful patron—in this case, the Inquisitor General of Venice—to secure legal protection, financial support, and religious approval for the book's contents.</meta>

# TO THE VERY REVEREND
# FATHER IN CHRIST,

## LORD GIOVANNI DOMENICO VIGNUCCI, <note>original: *Jo. Dominico Vignutio*</note>
### *Master of Sacred Theology, and Inquisitor*
### *General of Venice.*

->Roberto Mejetto sends greetings.<- <note>original: *S.* stands for *Salutem*, a standard greeting in Latin correspondence. Roberto Mejetto was a prominent Venetian printer and publisher.</note>

<image-desc>A square woodcut decorative initial 'S' containing an ornate pattern of curling vines and floral elements.</image-desc>

**I**f my own humble status were a match for your most ample virtues, many more distinguished monuments of my respect toward you would surely exist. For your modesty, your gravity, and your knowledge of both divine and human matters possess such power to inspire affection, that anyone who does not love you with a singular devotion or honor you with the highest respect must be entirely lacking in humanity or utterly devoid of wisdom. I have looked up to you ever since that day when I heard most learned men in my bookshop <term>bibliopolio<gloss>a shop where books were sold and often where intellectual discourse took place</gloss></term> celebrating the praise of your integrity, your preeminent virtue, and your labors. I have admired your merits toward the Christian Commonwealth <note>original: *Christianam Remp.* (Republic/Commonwealth); referring to the collective body of Western Christendom</note> which you achieve daily in routing the enemies of the holy and orthodox faith, as well as the wonderful diligence and skill you employ in this task. Why should I depend on the judgment of others regarding your praises and merits, when I have experienced for myself the sweetness of your character and the elegance of your words and manners?

<meta>catchword: maxima</meta>

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication by the publisher Roberto Mejetto to Giovanni Domenico Vignucci, the Inquisitor General of Venice. Mejetto praises Vignucci's character and his active role in defending the Catholic faith against heresy.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Giovanni Domenico Vignucci, Roberto Mejetto, Venice, Inquisition, Christian Commonwealth, bookshop, patronage</keywords>

# PREFACE

...combined with the greatest innocence of life: when you encouraged me, just as you did other printers <term>Typographos<gloss>literally 'typographers'; those responsible for the mechanical production and publication of books</gloss></term>, toward the correction and selection of books, so that nothing might be published that was not thoroughly polished and highly useful to the Commonwealth <note>original: *Reip.* (Republic/Commonwealth); here referring to the collective Christian society or the state of Venice</note>. This advice indeed settled so deeply in my heart that, from then on, I considered it not a small gain but a great profit that I obeyed your most wise counsel and satisfied you abundantly in this matter. 

Whether I have achieved this will be proven by this book on Exorcisms, which now comes to light once more, printed from my press. It is dedicated by me, knowingly and prudently, to you—that is, to the greatness of your virtues. For I have omitted nothing so that it might be read as complete in every respect <note>original: *omnibus numeris absolutus*, a Latin idiom meaning perfect or finished in all its parts</note>; I wish all who hold it in their hands to know by this testimony, however small it is, that I am most obedient to you. I have also seen the great reason to dedicate this little book—which assists those possessed by demons <term>Dæmonibus<gloss>evil spirits or fallen angels who were believed to inhabit human bodies</gloss></term> and those oppressed by evil spells <note>original: *maleficiis*; specifically referring to harm caused by witchcraft or sorcery</note>—to you, who care most deeply that souls deceived by diabolical fraud might return to health. 

Farewell. January 1, 1606 <note>original: *Kal. Januarii, M DC VI.* The Kalends was the first day of the month in the Roman calendar.</note>

He presents this collection of exorcism rituals as a tool for spiritual healing, dedicated to the Inquisitor as a sign of professional and religious devotion.</summary>

# FRIAR SANCTUS MELARANCIUS OF MONTE
## Rubiano,
### Of the Order of Friars Minor Conventual, to the Reader,

->Sends Greetings.<-
<note>original: *S. D.* (Salutem Dicit); a standard Latin greeting formula.</note>

Guided by delight in his frequent academic pursuits, with diligent labor as his companion in organizing all things, and with a readiness and natural brilliance of mind, the Reverend Valerius Polidorus of the Padua Convent—a Franciscan <term>Franciscanus<gloss>a member of the Order of Friars Minor, founded by St. Francis of Assisi</gloss></term> and a Doctor of Arts and Sacred Theology—has progressed so laudably in all areas that he is most clearly seen as marvelous in them. For just as he is friendly and upright in social conversation, so too is he solemn and eloquent in sermons; accessible and learned in his lectures; precise and clear in scholarly debates; and in the performance of Exorcisms <term>Exorcizationibus<gloss>the formal religious rites used to expel demons or evil spirits</gloss></term>, he always appears fearsome and effective. Do you not believe me? Then believe his works: look to the practice itself; and for now, consider his *Practice of Exorcists* with careful attention. See how, while he rebukes the Devil, he displays a solemn eloquence; while he presents the sacred mysteries—by whose power he works—he displays an accessible teaching style; and while he weakens the enemy by driving home his points more deeply, seeking out precise distinctions, you may witness his fearsome efficacy and derive many useful things. Indeed, this book is useful for Theologians, and especially for Preachers and Exorcists. For if you are a Theologian, you will find here the resolutions of divine mysteries; if you are a Preacher, many applicable points for your sermons will not be lacking; and if you are an Exorcist, you will undoubtedly be made perfect in your craft by this work. So that all the aforementioned things might be known more clearly, I have taken care to present them in the following indexes, which I have compiled:

<vocab>Friar Sanctus Melarancius, Valerius Polidorus, Franciscan, Exorcism, Practice of Exorcists, Theology, Preacher</vocab>

<summary>A commendatory preface by Friar Sanctus Melarancius praising Valerius Polidorus for his academic brilliance, eloquence in preaching, and fearsome effectiveness as an exorcist. He recommends the book as an essential manual for theologians, preachers, and practitioners of exorcism alike.</summary>
<keywords>Valerius Polidorus, Sanctus Melarancius, Franciscan Order, exorcism manual, sacred theology, preaching, demonic rebuke, ecclesiastical education</keywords>

# C O P Y

The Excellent Lords, Heads of the Most Illustrious <term>Council of Ten<gloss>One of the primary governing bodies of the Republic of Venice, responsible for state security and censorship</gloss></term>, whose names are written below, having received assurance from the <term>Reformers of the University of Padua<gloss>The board of Venetian officials responsible for overseeing education and the licensing of books</gloss></term>, based on the report of the two men deputized for this purpose: namely, the Reverend Father Inquisitor and the Distinguished Secretary of the Senate, Zuanne Maravegia, with an oath that in the booklet titled *The Practice of Exorcists* by Friar Valerius Polidorus of Padua, of the Franciscan Conventuals, for the expulsion of demons and sorcery, nothing is found contrary to the laws and it is worthy of printing, we grant license that it may be printed in this City.
Dated the 15th of November, 1605.

Lord Lorenzo Loredan. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; } &nbsp;&nbsp; Heads of the Most Illustrious
Lord Andrea Minotto. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; } &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Council of Ten.
Lord Z. da Leze. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; }

->Most Illustrious Council of Ten, Secretary Leonardo<-
->Ottobon,<-

->November 17, 1605.<-
->Registered in the book at page 137.<-

->Antonio Loredan, from the Office against Blasphemy.<-
<note>original: "Off. Con. Blasp." (Officiali sopra la Bestemmia); a Venetian magistracy that also oversaw the printing of books to ensure religious orthodoxy.</note>

Concerning all things to be treated in order in the Preface, 1
Concerning the firmness of faith required in an <term>Exorcist<gloss>A member of the clergy specifically trained and authorized to perform the rite of expelling demons</gloss></term>, 2

How and what should be asked of Demons <note>Strict rules governed what an exorcist could ask a demon; usually, questions were limited to the demon's name and the cause of possession to aid in their expulsion.</note>, 10
How, through the name of the principal besieging Spirit, the most powerful knowledge for the liberation of the <term>obsessed<gloss>original: "obsessi"; while "demoniac" implies internal possession, "obsessed" often refers to a person besieged or harassed by a demon from the outside</gloss></term> is discovered, 12

Concerning the nature of the Demon from things reported by various authors, 18

# INDEX OF PASSAGES

### *Reading from the prophet Isaiah, chapters 26 & 27.*

Awake and praise, etc. <note>original: "Expergiscimini, & laudate"; Isaiah 26:19, a passage concerning the resurrection of the dead and God's triumph over death, often used to symbolize spiritual restoration.</note>

### *Reading from the prophet Daniel, chapter 14.*

The King came with Daniel, etc. <note>original: "Venit Rex cum Daniele"; from the story of Bel and the Dragon, where Daniel proves the idols are false and destroys a great dragon worshipped as a god.</note>

Jesus, full of the Holy Spirit, returned from the Jordan and was led by the Spirit into the desert for forty days, and was tempted by the Devil. And he ate nothing during those days, and when they were finished, he was hungry. And the Devil said to him: "If you are the Son of God, tell this stone to become bread." And Jesus answered him: "It is written, that man does not live by bread alone, but by every word of God." <note>original: "non in pane solo vivit homo"; this quote from Deuteronomy is used by Jesus to prioritize spiritual sustenance over physical needs.</note> And the Devil led him onto a high mountain and showed him all the kingdoms of the world in a moment of time, and said to him: "I will give you all this power, and their glory, for they have been delivered to me, and I give them to whom I will. If you, therefore, falling down, will worship before me, all shall be yours." And Jesus, answering, said to him: "It is written: You shall worship the Lord your God, and him only shall you serve." And he led him to Jerusalem and set him upon the <term>pinnacle</term> <note>The highest point or wing of the Temple complex.</note> of the temple, and said to him: "If you are the Son of God, throw yourself down from here; for it is written, that he has given his angels charge over you, that they may preserve you, and that in their hands they shall bear you up, lest perhaps you strike your foot against a stone." And Jesus, answering, said to him: "It is said, you shall not tempt the Lord your God." And when all the temptation was finished, the devil departed from him.

### *Reading from the Holy Gospel according to Luke: chapter 4.*

Jesus went down into Capernaum, a city of Galilee, and there he taught them on the <term>sabbaths</term>. <note>The Jewish day of rest; the text uses the plural "sabbatis," indicating a period of ongoing ministry.</note> And they were astonished at his teaching, because his word was with power. And in the synagogue there was a man having an unclean <term>demon</term>, <note>original: "Dæmonium immundum"</note> and he cried out with a loud voice, saying: "Let us alone! What have we to do with you, Jesus of Nazareth? Have you come to destroy us? I know who you are, the Holy One of God." And Jesus rebuked him, saying: "Be silent, and come out of him." And when the demon had thrown him down into the midst, he came out of him and did him no harm. And fear came upon them all, and they talked among themselves, saying: "What is this word, for with power and authority he commands the unclean spirits, and they come out?" And his fame spread into every place of the region.

# PREFACE
## ON THE PRACTICE
### Of Exorcists.

->FRIAR VALERIUS POLIDORUS<-
->of Padua, of the Order of Friars Minor Conventual,<- <note>A branch of the Franciscan Order. Friars Minor translates to "Lesser Brothers," reflecting their vow of humility.</note>
->Doctor of Arts & Sacred Theology.<-

->Concerning all things to be treated in the Preface, in order. Chapter I.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate floral and foliate patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

<margin>
Summary of
the entire
preface.
</margin>

THERE ARE TWO areas in which the <term>Exorcist</term> <note>At the time this was written, "Exorcist" was one of the "Minor Orders" of the Catholic Church, a specific office leading toward the priesthood.</note> must exercise prudent attention: the first of these concerns himself; the second, however, concerns another. Regarding himself, one thing must be maintained and another must be rejected. That which is to be maintained is twofold: the first regards the firmness of faith; the second, the purity of conscience. That which is to be rejected is also twofold: the first is greed, and the second is vainglory. Furthermore, the second area, which concerns another, is also twofold: the first concerns the <term>possessed person</term> <note>original: "obsessum"; literally "the besieged," referring to the person suffering from demonic influence.</note> to be liberated, and the second concerns the <term>possessor</term> <note>original: "obsidens"; the demon occupying the person.</note> to be put to flight. Regarding the possessed person, there are two requirements: first, that fasting be observed; and second, that prayer be offered. This first requirement of fasting is twofold: one is from sins, and the other is from food and drink. The second, namely Prayer, can also be twofold: one which is directed toward God, and another toward the Saints. Finally, the second area, which concerns putting the possessing spirit to flight, is likewise twofold. The first of these relates indirectly to the aforementioned flight, while the second relates to it directly. That which relates indirectly is divided into seven points. The first of these is: Why the Devil attacks and enters human bodies. The second is: how it may be known whether someone is so [possessed]... 

<summary>This page introduces the Preface to Valerius Polidorus's manual, outlining the moral and spiritual preparations required of an exorcist. It categorizes the duties into self-discipline (faith and purity) and the care of the sufferer through prayer and fasting.</summary>
<keywords>Valerius Polidorus, exorcism manual, spiritual discipline, fasting, prayer, demonic possession, Franciscan theology, clerical duties</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic breakdown of the Preface begun on the previous page, detailing the specific points of inquiry for the exorcist before moving into Chapter II, which focuses on the necessity of faith.</meta>

...one who is demon-possessed, or <term>bewitched</term> <note>original: "maleficiatus"; refers to a person suffering from "maleficium," or harmful magic/witchcraft believed to be caused by a human agent in league with a demon.</note>. The third point is how and what should be asked of demons. The fourth is how, through the name of the principal spirit, the knowledge of the most powerful means of liberating the possessed person may be discovered. The fifth concerns the various names of the Devil. The sixth concerns the nature of the Demon, to be understood from those things collected by the <term>Master of Sentences</term> <note>A reference to Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose "Four Books of Sentences" was the standard theological textbook of the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>. And the seventh concerns the nature of the Demon, to be understood from what is reported by various authors. Finally, those things that are immediately relevant are five in number, according to the five senses in the human body, through all of which the Devil is to be tormented and witchcraft destroyed. The first concerns things that can be **Touched**. The second, things that can be **Seen**. The third, things that can be **Smelled**. The fourth, things that can be **Tasted**. And the fifth, things that can be **Heard**. Regarding all of these, and according to this order, a specifically exact account is to be completed successively here, and the extension of the Preface concluded.

### *On the firmness of faith, which is required in the Exorcist. Chapter II.*

<margin>
Faith is required in the Exorcist.
</margin>
Arming those who must struggle *against Principalities and Powers, and against the rulers of the world of this darkness*, the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in his Letter to the Ephesians.</note> commends the **Shield of faith** above all other types of armor when he says: *In all things taking the shield of faith, wherewith you may be able to extinguish all the fiery darts of the most wicked one.* <note>original: "Eph. 6"; referencing Ephesians 6:16.</note> From this, first, it is very easily understood that the Exorcist must be most faithful regarding all things to be believed, as handed down by the Holy Roman Catholic and Apostolic Church, since he himself is about to fight against the snares of the Devil. More clearly, however, the Lord declares the same purpose in the Gospel; <note>original: "Matth. 17"; referencing Matthew 17:19-20.</note> who, answering his disciples when they asked why they could not cast out a demon from a certain youth, and saying: *Why could we not cast him out?* He said: *Because of your unbelief.* <note>original: "Propter incredulitatem vestram."</note> Therefore, let firm faith be in him, since it is the firm foundation for putting the Demon to flight.

<summary>This page concludes the preface's outline—detailing how the five senses are used to combat demonic influence—and begins Chapter II, which emphasizes that an exorcist's primary weapon is unwavering faith in Catholic doctrine.</summary>
<keywords>five senses, Peter Lombard, Master of Sentences, Shield of Faith, spiritual warfare, Ephesians 6, Matthew 17, unbelief, bewitched</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface's instructions for the exorcist, moving from the requirement of faith discussed at the end of the previous page to the necessity of moral purity and the dangers of greed.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
-> P R E F A C E <-
<sig>A 2</sig>

## On the purity of conscience to be sought by the Exorcist. Chapter III.

<margin>The principal and ministerial cause of the liberation of the possessed.</margin>
<margin>The Exorcist ought to abandon sin.</margin>
All Exorcists who have existed until now have declared that the liberation of a demon-possessed person must be sought first from God the blessed, as the **principal cause**, and then from the Exorcist as the **ministerial cause** <note>In scholastic philosophy, the "principal cause" is the primary source of an action (God), while the "ministerial cause" is the instrument or agent through whom God works (the priest).</note>. They have left behind their two-fold conjurations, placing within them both prayers and exorcisms: prayers, so that the person might be liberated by God; and exorcisms, so that they might be liberated by the exorcist through the authority handed down to him by the Lord. This is found in Luke, chapter 9, where it says the Lord gave the Apostles power and authority over all Demons. 

However, God and the Exorcist relate to the aforementioned liberation in such a way that God, even if the exorcist is unwilling, can perform such a liberation; but the exorcist cannot do so if God is unwilling. Therefore, one must knock upon the goodness of God with tireless prayers, so that He might be made a favorer to the exorcist and those with him (even as the person is in such a state), and so that, having his prayers heard, he may more easily obtain the intended liberation. 

How, though, will he be heard more easily if he has not cast off his sins? If he is not pure and clean of conscience? Indicating this, David said: *If I have looked upon iniquity in my heart, the Lord will not hear me* <note>original: "Iniquitatem si aspexi in corde meo non exaudiet Dominus." Psalm 65:18 in the Vulgate, corresponding to Psalm 66:18 in modern translations.</note>. Therefore, a good exorcist must be pure and bright in conscience—at the very least through true <term>contrition</term> <note>Sincere sorrow for one's sins.</note> when a <term>confessor</term> <note>A priest authorized to hear confessions.</note> is not readily available, and through a complete confession of all his sins whenever it is possible—for this reason above all.

## On the Greed to be avoided by the Exorcist. Chapter IV.

<margin>Paul the Apostle liberated a girl from a Pythonic spirit.</margin>
A certain girl having a <term>Pythonic spirit</term> <note>original: "spiritum Pythonem"; a spirit of divination or fortune-telling, named after the "Python" of the Delphic Oracle.</note>, who provided great profit to her masters by divining, had followed Paul and those who were with him for many days as they went to prayer, and had cried out daily, saying: *These men are servants of the most high God, who announce to you the way of salvation;*

<summary>This page establishes that while God is the ultimate healer, the exorcist serves as a vital secondary instrument who must remain free from sin to be effective. It begins to warn against greed by citing the biblical account of a slave girl possessed by a spirit of divination.</summary>

<page-num>4</page-num>
-> P R E F A C E , <-

from the spirit; that she received liberation from possession by Paul is clearly apparent from the Acts of the Apostles, chapter 16. Moreover, from the aforementioned passage, I gather that this was done by Paul for two reasons. The first is because, being composed of holy modesty,
<margin>The Exorcist must avoid greed.</margin>
he did not wish to be publicly praised for such perfections by a Pythonic spirit <note>original: "spiritu Pythonico." In the ancient world, "Pythonic" referred to the spirit of the Pythia at Delphi, used here to mean a spirit of divination or prophecy.</note>. The second reason is because the aforementioned girl provided a great, yet wicked, profit to her masters; therefore, he judged that such profits of greed should not be permitted to continue. From this, the exorcist can gather that he must not use the Devil to acquire temporal goods; for he would commit such a crime if he were to expect <term>Simoniacal gifts</term> <note>Simony is the ecclesiastical crime of paying for or selling holy items or spiritual services, named after Simon Magus from the New Testament.</note> in exchange for procuring the demon's flight. Therefore, he shall not desire this for himself, lest he offend God, nor shall he seek it by gesture, word, deed, or any other manner, lest he similarly offend God and become a scandal to his neighbor. For this same
<margin>How the exorcist may receive gifts.</margin>
reason, he would neither be heard as easily by God, nor be as intensely feared by the Devil. It must be noted, however, that if he should receive something for necessary alms from those giving freely—provided he does so without a stain of the soul in his desiring, or any outward error in his requesting—this would be with God’s blessing. Yet, in all these matters, one must proceed cautiously and according to a knowing conscience.

### *On the Vainglory to be Avoided by the Exorcist.*
### Chapter V.

<margin>Let the exorcist avoid empty glory.</margin>
If an Exorcist wishes to boast because of the performance of his office and the effect that followed, it is because he considers such a performance or effect to be a good, perfect, or excellent thing. If this is so, he must first remember that every excellent gift and every perfect gift comes from above, descending from the Father of lights, as Saint James testifies. And so, he should have offered honor and glory only to the immortal
<margin>Christ did not want the disciples to rejoice in the casting out of the Devil.</margin>
and invisible King of ages. Secondly, our Lord Jesus Christ favors this sentiment; when the seventy returned to him with joy, saying: "Lord, even the Demons are subject to us in your name," as seen in Luke, chapter 10.

<summary>This page warns exorcists against the sins of greed and vainglory, using the Apostle Paul's refusal to profit from a possessed girl as a primary moral example. It emphasizes that the power to cast out demons is a gift from God, and therefore all credit belongs to the divine rather than the human practitioner.</summary>

[He] responded: Do not rejoice in this, that the spirits are subject to you <note>original: "in hoc nolite gaudere, quia spiritus subjiciuntur vobis," referencing the Gospel of Luke 10:20.</note>. If He did not wish the Disciples to rejoice, how could they boast? The first [vainglory] did not please Him; therefore, the second [pride] in this work does not please Him either. Therefore, let the <term>exorcist</term> <note>An ordained minister or person authorized by the Church to perform the rite of driving out demons.</note> pursue his work humbly in all things, and without the winds of vainglory.

### *Concerning the twofold Fast to be observed by the possessed. Chapter VI.*

<margin>A twofold fast must be observed by the possessed person.</margin>
The Lord testifies that fasting is required in the expulsion of demons from <term>possessed</term> <note>original: "obsessis," literally "besieged" or "occupied" by a spirit.</note> bodies, saying to his disciples when they asked why they could not cast out the demon: *This kind can come out by nothing but prayer and fasting* <note>Mark 9:29.</note>. This should be observed especially by the possessed person, if it is possible. Moreover, the fast can be twofold: The first is from sins, and this is sanctified when, after a complete confession of one's sins, he casts out the occasion of sinning for the future. Furthermore, it is required of the <term>demoniac</term> <note>The historical term for a person suffering from demonic possession.</note> that divine goodness be more easily obtained, whether by the person himself, or by the Exorcist, or by those standing in for him. The second fast, however, is from food and drink, by which the demons are greatly irritated, greatly weakened, and most easily overcome. We have the example of the Savior, who, about to fight with the Devil in the desert, fasted for forty days and forty nights, as found in Matthew 4 and Luke 4. This twofold fast must therefore be observed so that, for the stated reasons, the cure of the possessed person may be more easily obtained.

### *Concerning the Prayer of the possessed person, to be directed toward God and the Saints. Chapter VII.*

<margin>The intercession of the saints must be requested by the possessed.</margin>
The Disciples asked our Lord Jesus Christ on behalf of the Canaanite woman, who had come out from the borders of Tyre and Sidon, whose daughter was troubled by a demon; but she also asked the same Lord for the recovery of her daughter’s health, wherefore she finally heard that word of the Lord: *Be it done to you as you desire* <note>Matthew 15:28.</note>. From this, I have decided that the possessed person should be warned about the twofold prayer to be performed; the first of which is made to all the saints and

## *Why the Devil attacks and enters human bodies. Chapter VIII.*

<margin>Points on which the <term>demoniac</term> <note>A person suffering from demonic possession.</note> should be advised; a person is troubled by a Demon for many reasons.</margin>
Those words—by which a possessed person indicates they think themselves abandoned by God when they find themselves handed over to demonic <term>possession</term> <note>original: "obsidioni" – literally a "siege," referring to the demon's external and internal assault on the person.</note>—do not necessarily come from a lack of confidence in God’s goodness. However, the evil spirits, drawing encouragement from such thoughts, rage all the more fiercely. Therefore, to uproot such a harmful opinion, the <term>Exorcist</term> <note>A priest or official authorized to perform the rite of driving out demons.</note> must know how to advise the possessed person that a demonic invasion is not a certain sign of God's anger. Indeed, there can be many causes for it, and primarily a fivefold one: the first of which is because of a doubt in the faith...

<meta>This page concludes Chapter VIII, detailing the five theological reasons why God allows demonic possession, and begins Chapter IX, which advises caution in diagnosing possession versus natural illness. It follows the previous page’s discussion on the spiritual preparation of the sufferer.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

<margin>Points on which the <term>demoniac</term> <note>A person suffering from demonic possession.</note> should be advised; why a person is troubled by a Demon...</margin>

...to be removed; for there are some who deny that Demons exist, whom the Lord permits to be troubled by a Demon in a bodily and sensible way in order to warn them. To such non-believers, that word of the Lord can be applied: *Unless you see signs and wonders, you do not believe* <note>John 4:48</note>. 

The second reason is for the punishment of someone’s grave sin; thus we read in 1 Kings 16 and 18 <note>original: "1. Reg. 16. & 18." In historical Catholic Bibles, 1 & 2 Kings refer to 1 & 2 Samuel.</note> that Saul was possessed by a Demon because of his disobedience. And we know that Paul delivered a certain Corinthian to Satan to be troubled bodily (1 Corinthians 5), who, having been warned to withdraw from carnal relations with his stepmother, refused. 

The third reason is for the increase of grace and merit in the one possessed; as is clear regarding Job, whom the Lord permitted to be treated harshly by a Demon. 

The fourth reason is for the manifestation of someone’s holiness; hence, to show the perfection of the holy Apostle Peter, God wills that frequently, by the mere touch of the chains with which he had been bound under Herod, those possessed by a Demon are delivered, so that it may be verified that *God is wonderful in his saints* <note>Psalm 68:35</note>. 

The fifth reason is to declare the power of divine operation; for when Demons are driven far away in the name of the Lord, his power is recognized as greater than the power of the Devil, even though it is said of the Devil’s power that *there is no power on earth that can be compared to him* <note>Job 41:24</note>. The Lord’s saying in John 9 declares this reason: *Neither did this man sin, nor his parents, but that the works of God might be manifested in him*. 

For these reasons and causes, therefore, the possessed person must be effectively warned to have constant faith and hope in the divine mercy and goodness to be attained.

...those burdened with bodily ailments may be believed to be possessed by demons, <margin>The sick: care must be taken lest they be believed to be possessed by demons.</margin> or afflicted by witchcraft; for whom the dignity of the office and the authority of God must be poured out, though only after serious deliberation. Therefore, one must first inquire most diligently whether such a person is seized by a Demon; and afterwards, whether it is through witchcraft <note>original: "maleficium"</note>. <margin>A probable sign for recognizing a demoniac.</margin> The first can be conveniently determined by the following three signs; the first of which is based on probability, and it is this: if, upon the laying on of some holy Relic over the possessed <note>original: "obsessum," literally "one who is beset"</note>, or a blessed object, while the person is unaware; or the laying on of holy hands; the person should detest holy things, or the sacraments, and the like; or if, because of certain strict commands and holy words, he is seen to shudder and tremble, or suffer sudden pains, or unusual twisting <note>original: "torsiones"</note>, or raging movements; it will be a sign of a Diabolical presence. <margin>A rational sign for recognizing a demoniac.</margin> The second sign is based on reason; which is if the possessed speaks in the tongue of foreign nations; if he speaks of difficult and highly learned matters although he is an uneducated person <note>original: "idiota"</note>; if he reveals hidden things; if he announces truly remote events; and if he knows something said to someone in the ear in silence; the same judgment should be drawn. <margin>Gospel signs for recognizing a demoniac.</margin> The third sign indeed is from the Gospel; and it is that sometimes blindness is caused in the patient by evil spirits, as is clear in Matthew 12; likewise sometimes Deafness, in Mark 9. Sometimes an impediment in the tongue for speaking, in Matthew chapters 9, 10, and 12. Sometimes the gnashing of teeth, in Mark 9. Sometimes the emitting of foam from the mouth, in Mark 9. Sometimes a cry, in Mark 5. Sometimes a stooped gait and a downcast face, in Luke 9. The possessed sometimes suffer from the phases of the Moon and appear to be lunatics, in Matthew 17. Sometimes they strip off their clothes, in Luke 9. Sometimes they cut themselves with stones, in Mark 5. Sometimes they fall into fire or into water, in Matthew 17 and Luke 9. Sometimes they flee from home and the company of men, in Mark 5 and Luke 8. Sometimes they waste away, in Mark 9. Sometimes they fall ill, in Luke 13. Sometimes they rage excessively, in Matthew 8. And finally, they are tormented in many ways, in Matthew 15 and 17, and Luke 9. <margin>Intrinsic and extrinsic signs of witchcraft.</margin> These things were first to be said concerning the signs of a demon-possessed person. Afterwards, therefore, it is said regarding the signs of one who is bewitched <note>original: "maleficiati," referring to those harmed by the "maleficium" or "evil doing" of a witch</note> that there are two principal ones. The first is external; the second is internal. The external is rumor;

...because sometimes those afflicted by witchcraft <note>original: "maleficiati," referring to people suffering from harmful magic</note> seem to swell up in every part of their body. At other times, they appear excessively exhausted in every other movement of the body, so much so that they are completely hindered from moving at all, just as usually happens because of swelling. Sometimes, however, and in most cases, they have a sallow, cedar-colored complexion <note>original: "cedrinum," likely referring to a sickly yellowish or pale wood-like skin tone</note> (especially in the face); and they suffer many different types of torments in all their limbs.

Internally, signs of witchcraft appear around the heart, the stomach, and the belly. First, regarding the heart: those afflicted feel as though the heart is being constricted, or stung, or gnawed as if by dogs. Second, regarding the stomach: it seems to them that the opening of the stomach <note>original: "os ventriculi," often used historically to refer to the cardia or the pit of the stomach</note> is being squeezed; they do not digest their food, even when a remedy is applied by doctors, but instead vomit whatever they have taken. To some, it seems that a lump <note>original: "bolus"</note> rises and falls from the stomach, though not always immediately; they try to swallow it but cannot, though eventually it descends on its own. Third, regarding the belly: they suffer the most bitter twistings <note>original: "torsiones," a term for cramping or griping pains</note>. Sometimes it seems to them that a freezing wind is rushing through the belly; at other times, a most burning vapor, like a flame of fire. Concerning these, see chapters 15, 17, and 18 below for remedies involving the senses of touch, smell, and taste. Nevertheless, all these things must be weighed with diligent investigation.

### *How and what should be asked of Demons. Chapter 10.*

<margin>The goal of the exorcistic art.</margin>
To understand how error might occur in the petitions <note>requests or demands</note> made to the besieging Demon—and how it might not—it is necessary to know the purpose of this art. The purpose <note>original: "finis"</note> is the desire to repel the Demon from ourselves and our neighbors by divine power and the authority granted to us, so that they may no longer do harm. Given this, it is said first that error can occur in two ways when making interrogations. One way concerns the method <margin>Error occurs in two ways.</margin> or the means directed toward the aforementioned goal; the other way concerns the goal itself. Regarding the method, it happens in two ways: first, when—

<page-num>11</page-num>
# PREFACE.

<margin>...regarding petitions, from those ordered toward the goal of the Exorcistic art.</margin>
...firstly, <note>The text begins mid-word with "-mo," completing "primo" (firstly) from the previous page.</note> when the manner of asking pertains to a kind of friendship or equality with the Demon; second, when the manner of asking pertains to friendship combined with the inferiority of the petitioner in respect to the Demon. But regarding the first, error can occur in two ways: namely, either **expressly**, such as by entreating or by trying to persuade the demon through reverence for something sacred; or **implicitly**, and this in two ways: either knowingly, as with Magic incantations and unknown names or characters <note>original: "characteribus incognitis," referring to occult symbols used in ritual magic</note>; or unknowingly, through prayers that are believed to be good but contain superstitious and vain elements. If, however, we look at the second [type of error], this is also two-fold: namely, either that we might learn something from the Devil as if from a Teacher, or that we might obtain something from him as if from a Lord.

<margin>Error can occur in two ways in petitions regarding the goal of this art.</margin>
In another way also, error can happen regarding the goal [of the exorcism]; and this in two ways. One way is by asking for things that *could* be directed toward the goal, but are not [actually] so directed—as when questioning occurs about the name of the spirit, or his rank, or his companions, out of mere curiosity. In another way, by asking for things that *cannot* be directed toward the aforesaid goal—as when a question might be asked about some image found while digging in a field <note>original: "suffossione agri," referring to archaeological discovery or finding buried "charms"</note>, such as whether it belongs to Venus or Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom</note> and such things.

<margin>Error does not occur in petitions if two things are observed.</margin>
I say secondly that error will not occur in making interrogations if two things are observed. The first of these is that the manner or the means directed toward the proper goal be by way of **commanding**, and without superstition, as has been cautioned. The second is that all things be immediately or ultimately directed toward the proper goal. With all these things assumed and observed, I say first in general that every petition for which an occasion arises can be made lawfully.

<margin>There are usually ten petitions to be made to Demons.</margin>
I say second, in particular, that the following petitions are usually made. The first concerns the **name**, so that once it is known, it may be written and placed as a title above a drawing of the Demon <note>original: "effigiandum Dæmonem," the practice of creating a physical representation of the spirit to gain power over it</note> on paper, to be burned, as is explained below in Chapter 16. And this is done so that the sacred words, in which he is expressed by name, may trouble him even more; and following the example of Christ, who [asked] about the

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of legitimate reasons for an exorcist to interrogate a demon, following the discussion on page 11 regarding questions directed toward the goal of liberation rather than idle curiosity.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
# PREFACE.

...[the Lord] sometimes asked the [demon's] **name**, as is found in Luke 8 and Mark 5 <note>original: "nomine aliquando quæſivit," referring to Jesus asking the demon "What is your name?" and receiving the answer "Legion."</note>. And for other ends ordered toward the aforementioned principal goal. The **second** interrogation will be about his companions and henchmen, and their number, so that they too may be commanded. The **third**, about the cause of entry, so that a contrary remedy may be applied. The **fourth**, if there is a curse <term>maleficium: a physical object or spell used by a witch to invite a demon into a person</term>, so that appropriate Exorcisms may be read. The **fifth**, about the manner of destroying the curse, so that it may be more carefully rooted out. The **sixth**, which order of Angels, or choir, or which saint he fears most, so that they may be invoked for help. The **seventh**, which sacred words he most abhors, so that they may be repeated. The **eight**, which Demon he has as a most bitter enemy and rival, so that he may be summoned against him. The **ninth**, by which Exorcist he is to be expelled, so that the divine judgment may be proved. The **tenth**, on which day or hour he fears he must depart, so that more fervent efforts may be made then. And things of this sort. 

One must, however, beware of curious digressions, and not simply believe the responses, even if one should not omit remedies known only through those answers, which can indeed be helpful and in no way harmful. One must also beware of the spontaneous, frequent, and deceitful speech of the Demon; indeed, they should sometimes be forbidden from speaking, following the example of the Savior saying: *Be silent, and come out of him* <note>original: "Obmuteſce, & exi ab eo."</note> (Mark 1 and Luke 4). This is also confirmed by that passage in Luke 4, which says that Jesus, rebuking the Demons, did not allow them to speak. Thus, one must proceed in these petitions in this manner.

### *How, through the name of the principal besieging spirit, knowledge of the most powerful means of liberating the possessed person is discovered. Chapter XI.*

Just as there are certain Demons assigned to one province, and others to one City, and some indeed assigned only to one man; so some are found who preside over a single vice, because they are especially powerful at tempting men with that vice. Hence it is that not infrequently, at the same time, in various possessed people, several evil spirits are found,

<meta>This page continues the Preface's discussion on the interrogation of demons, specifically focusing on the significance of the demon's name and how an exorcist should proceed if a name is withheld or revealed. It then transitions into Chapter 12.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
# PREFACE.

<margin>Why different spirits are called by the same name.</margin>
<margin>The name of the principal spirit must be sought.</margin>
<margin>A name must sometimes be imposed upon the spirits.</margin>
...who are called by a single name, for this arises from the same duty of tempting or from their very nature. For they do not possess proper names <note>In demonology, "proper names" usually refer to unique personal identities; here, the author suggests demons are often defined by their functions.</note>, but rather names imposed based on their office or nature. Therefore, when any possessed person <term>Dæmoniacus: an individual tormented or controlled by a demon</term> comes into the hands of the Exorcist, the name of the principal spirit must be sought with the greatest diligence. Moreover, a name of this kind will either be revealed by the responding spirit through the power of God, or it will not be revealed due to some judgment of the Most High known to us. If it is not revealed, a vile and reproachful name must be imposed upon him, such as: "Fountain of Vices," "Father of Lies," "Most Beastly Beast," and the like; he shall be addressed and written down by such a name. However, the proceedings to be taken against him should follow the common method of Exorcising.

<margin>What to do if the revealed name of the spirit is famous.</margin>
If, however, the name is revealed and is famous from Holy Scripture or elsewhere, its nature or office will be understood through its meaning; and consequently, the vice over which it has power will be known. For this reason, it will be most useful to employ those virtues that are immediately contrary to such a vice. If, for example, an evil spirit is found who is called Mammon <note>original: "Mammona," a term for wealth or greed found in the New Testament</note>, which according to the Syrians means "wealth" <note>original: "divitiæ dicitur"</note>, and for that reason he is caught using wealth to deceive men, one must stand against him especially through the embrace of poverty and through alms—even those mercifully distributed during the very act of conjuration <term>conjuratio: the formal command or oath used by an exorcist to compel a demon</term>.

<margin>What to do if the revealed name of the spirit is not famous.</margin>
If the name is revealed but is not famous or well-known, it should be interpreted by upright men according to its etymology in the Latin idiom, or Greek, or Hebrew, or some other language, or even according to a combination of these. He shall afterward be opposed in the same manner described for a known name. This will be (with the assistance of God’s grace) a most powerful means for the liberation of the Possessed <term>Obsessi: those besieged or occupied by a demonic presence</term>.

# PREFACE.

<margin>Holy Scripture, interpreted.</margin>
On account of certain occurring opportunities, I will recount some names of the Devil along with their interpretations. And first, I will speak of those names which are found in the Holy Scriptures; second, however, I will speak of those taken from elsewhere. Regarding the first, I say that he is principally called the **Devil** <note>original: *Diabolus*</note>; as is found in 1 Peter 5. This is interpreted as "flowing down," because he flowed down and fell from heaven; or it is interpreted as "Shut in a dungeon," because he is not permitted to cause as much harm as he would wish. Also, he is called a **Demon**, as found in John 8. This means "one who knows," because he possesses a threefold knowledge <note>In medieval theology, this "triple knowledge" usually refers to a demon's natural intelligence, their long experience of human history, and what they can learn from divine revelation.</note>. Also, he is called **Belial**, as found in Numbers 1. This is interpreted as "without a yoke," because by fighting against the Lord as much as he can, he refuses to be subject to Him. Likewise, **Beelzebub**, as found in Luke 11. This is interpreted as "man of flies," that is, of evil souls. He is called **Satan**, as found in 1 Peter 5, which means "Adversary," because he opposes God and us. He is called **Behemoth**, as found in Job 41, which means "Beast," because he always seeks to make men beastly. He is called **Leviathan**, as found in Isaiah 27, which means "addition," because he seeks for men to add sins upon sins. He is called **Mammon**, as found in Luke 19, which means "Riches," because he uses them to deceive men. He is called **Lucifer**, as found in Isaiah 14, which means "light-bringer," because he illuminates the minds of men toward evil. He is called the **Sinner**, as found in John 6, because he has sinned from the beginning and seeks to make us sin. He is called the **Exterminator** <note>original: *Exterminator*; typically translated as "Abaddon" or "Apollyon" in Revelation 9, meaning "The Destroyer."</note>, as found in Revelation 9, because he seeks to cast men outside the bounds of reason. He is called **Death**, as found in Revelation 11, because he was the author of death. He is called the **Eagle**, as found in Proverbs 30, because he flies high in malice so that he may not be understood by men. He is called the **Ox**, as found in Job 40, because he seeks to spiritually devour men, who are called "hay," according to that passage in Isaiah 40: "All flesh is grass [or hay]." He is called the **Giant**, as found in Job 16, because he is raised up proudly against God and us. He is called the **Lion**, as found in 1 Peter 5, because he seeks to spiritually devour men. He is called **Night**, as found in Psalm 18, because he seeks to obscure the minds of men—

He is called a **Servant**, as is found in Job 40. Because he is an unwilling servant of God, and he strives to make us serve both sin and himself. He is called a **Bolt** <note>original: *Vectis*, a bar used to lock a door</note>, as is found in Isaiah 27 and Psalm 106. Because by not resisting his promptings, we are shut up in a prison. These and similar names are those by which the evil angel is called, and they can belong not just to one, but to many, according to their shared office or nature.

## *On the Nature of the Demon, to be Learned from those things Collected by the Master of the Sentences.*

### Chapter XIII.

<margin>*The history, nature, and properties of the demon, after a fashion.*</margin>
So that the Exorcist may be more fully instructed in the Nature of the Demon and know where he ought to seek refuge when necessity urges, he must consider the passages of the most illustrious **Master of the Sentences** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160). His *Sentences* was the standard theological textbook for centuries</note>, especially in the Second Book, in which he discusses these matters most clearly. He should, however, carefully examine those articles in which the Master’s opinion is not commonly held <note>A warning that some of Lombard’s specific conclusions were debated or superseded by later scholastic theologians like Thomas Aquinas</note>. 

Therefore, if it should happen that he wants to know how **Lucifer** said (according to Isaiah 14): *I will ascend into heaven, and I will be like the Most High* <note>original: "Ascendam in coelum, & ero similis altissimo"</note>, even though he was already in Heaven, he should consult the Master, Book 2 of the *Sentences*, Distinction 2, Chapter 8. If he desires to know whether some among the greater and lesser spirits fell—among whom one was higher, namely Lucifer, as mentioned above—see Distinction 6, Chapter 1. If he wishes to know from where and to where he was cast down, see Chapter 2 as cited above. Whether he has been bound in hell since he tempted Christ and was defeated—he who they say tempted and conquered the first man—see Chapter 6 as cited above. Whether he has the power which he will have in the time of the Anti-

Whether **Demons** are creators, since through them **Magicians** produced frogs and other things <note>A reference to the magicians of Pharaoh in Exodus 8, who appeared to replicate some of Moses' miracles</note>; let him see as above, chapters 8, 9, and 10. ## Concerning the nature of the Demon, to be understood from what is reported by various authors.
### Chapter 14.

<margin>Many notable points for exorcists.</margin>
In addition to those things collected in the preceding chapter 13, there are many things to be known regarding the nature of the **Demon** <term>Dæmon: in this context, specifically a fallen angel or evil spirit</term>, which are reported by both theologians and philosophers. Those reported by theologians are especially noted here, and the locations for reading about them more extensively are marked. Therefore, if anyone desires to know how the **Devil** speaks to man, let him read Book 2, chapter 19 of the *Compendium of Theology*. Or if he desires to know the nature of the demons' malice, let him read as above, chapter 26. Or if he wishes to recognize what harms they inflict upon man and how they deceive them, let him read as above, chapter 27. In all these matters, let him not forget the book called the *Malleus Maleficarum* <note>original: "Hammer of Witches" (1486); the most famous medieval treatise on the detection and prosecution of witches</note>, in which demons and **witchcraft** <term>maleficium: harmful magic or "malefice" intended to cause injury</term> are treated at length.

I would furthermore speak of the reason why the devil enters human bodies, and of the method he employs (which is different than before), as well as concerning witchcraft; regarding the signs by which a bewitched person is recognized; regarding the conditions of the **Exorcist** <term>Exorcista: a member of the clergy specifically trained or ordained to perform the rite of expelling demons</term> and how he ought to prepare himself. I would speak of the signs that appear when the priest adjures <note>original: *conjurat*; to command a spirit solemnly under oath</note> the bewitched person; of the method the Devil uses to exit from bodies; of the deceptions he uses to trick the Exorcist; and other things necessary for this work of ours. However, because all these things are fully contained in the third part of the *Sacerdotal Book* <note>original: *lib. Sacerdotalis*; likely the *Sacerdotale* by Alberto Castellano, a popular 16th-century manual for priests</note>, after the *Compendium of Music*, I shall therefore pass over them in silence. It would remain for me to run through the opinions of the Philosophers as well, but perhaps that is not expedient at present.

<margin>Many notable points for theological exorcists.</margin>
Nevertheless, I provide the names of the Philosophers and the places in which something is discussed by them concerning the nature of the Demon, so that if there are things to be retained, they may be kept, and if things are to be omitted, they may be set aside. Therefore, the return...

-># PREFACE.<-

<margin>And Philosophers.</margin>
Therefore, the curious investigator of such matters will be able to find information in Plato in many places, but especially in the *Symposium*; in Proclus, in his commentary on Plato's *Alcibiades*; in Chalcidius, in his commentary on Plato’s *Timaeus*; in Porphyry, in the book *On Occasions* and in the book *On the Responses of the Philosophers*; in Iamblichus, in the book *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians, Chaldeans, and Assyrians*; in Aristotle, in the book *On the Wonders of the World*, because he mentions **Demons** <term>Dæmon: here referring to a wide range of spirits recognized by ancient philosophy</term> there, as reported by Apuleius; and in Averroes <note>The 12th-century Islamic philosopher Ibn Rushd, famous for his commentaries on Aristotle</note>, in his *Letter on the Resurrection*, in the book *On Sleep*, and in the book *Disputations*, Disputation 3. See also Lucius Apuleius the Physician, in the book *On the God of Socrates*; Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurium"</note>, in the *Pymander*, in the book *On the Power and Wisdom of God*; Plotinus, in the book *On One's Own Spirit*; Xenocrates, in the book *On Death*; Al-Ghazali <note>original: "Algazelem"</note>, in his *Metaphysics* and in the book *On the Soul*; Psellus, in the book *On Demons*; Eutychius, and Agostino Nifo <note>original: "Aug. Niphum"</note>, in the book *On Demons*; Gianfrancesco Pico, Count of Mirandola, in his book *The Witch* <note>original: "Strigiæ" (1523), an influential dialogue on the reality of witchcraft</note>. But also see Giovanni Lorenzo Anania of Taberna, in the book *On the Nature of Demons*; and Andrea Cesalpino of Arezzo, in the book *Peripatetic Investigation of Demons*; and finally all the Platonists, and many others whom I omit for the sake of brevity at present, as I hurry along with a running pen to other matters.

### Concerning those things pertaining to the flight of the Demon and the destruction of **witchcraft** through those things which are applied by touch. Chapter 15.
<term>Maleficium: harmful magic or "malefice" intended to cause injury or illness</term>

<margin>Things for the use of demoniacs must be blessed.</margin>
Holy Pope Alexander I, in his first Letter to the brothers throughout Egypt, taught by word and deed that things must be consecrated for both divine and human use, and especially applied to the sick <note>Alexander I (r. c. 107–115 AD) is traditionally credited with introducing the use of holy water mixed with salt for purifying Christian homes</note>. Since we must now take account of those things to be consecrated or blessed—specifically the things pertaining to touch that serve our current work—we must first determine the more principal matters, and later the subsequent ones.

<summary>This page provides a comprehensive bibliography of philosophical and Renaissance authorities on demonology before beginning a practical chapter on the use of blessed objects. It cites the early Church precedent of Pope Alexander I to justify the use of physical sacramentals in treating the "demoniac" or those afflicted by witchcraft.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the bibliography of philosophical authorities on the previous page to the practical application of exorcism rites. It introduces the physical materials used in the ritual—specifically water, oil, and salt—and begins the formal liturgical prayers for their consecration. The catchword "Exorcif-" on the previous page refers to the "Exorcismus Salis" (Exorcism of Salt) found here.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<margin>By which tangible things the devil may be expelled.</margin>
I say, therefore, that there are two principal things pertaining to the sense of touch. The first is **Water**; the second is **Oil**. Water is twofold: the first type pertains to sprinkling; the second to washing. Oil, however, is brought in for anointing. The sprinkling of Water ought to be performed over the possessed, the bewitched, and things to be blessed, when and how it will be stated in the appropriate places. Washing should then be undertaken by the **Bewitched** <term>Maleficiatis: those suffering from "maleficia" or harmful magic/spells</term> and the **Demoniacs** <term>Dæmoniacis: individuals believed to be inhabited by an evil spirit</term>, at the discretion of the Exorcist. Anointing with oil indeed ought to be applied to the Bewitched in whatever way shall be declared. But all these things must first be blessed. Therefore, the common blessing of Water is as follows:

->Verse: Our help is in the name of the Lord.<-
->Response: Who made heaven and earth.<-

### Exorcism of Salt.

> I exorcise you, creature of Salt, by the living † God, and by the true † God, by the holy † God, by the God who ordered you to be cast into the water by **Elisha the Prophet** <note>Reference to 2 Kings 2:19–22, where the prophet Elisha purified a spring at Jericho by throwing salt into it</note> so that the sterility of the water might be healed; so that you may become exorcised Salt for the salvation of believers, and that you may be health of soul and body to all who take you; and let every phantasm, wickedness, and the cunning of Diabolical fraud, and every unclean spirit, flee and depart from the place where you have been sprinkled, being adjured by him who is coming to judge the living and the dead, and the world by fire. Amen.

### Prayer.

> We humbly implore your immense clemency, almighty eternal God, that you might deign in your piety to **bless** † and **sanctify** † this creature of Salt, which you have granted for the use of the human race, so that it may be health of mind and body to all who take it; and let whatever is touched or sprinkled by it be free from all uncleanness and from every attack of spiritual wickedness. Through our Lord Jesus Christ your Son, who lives and reigns with you, etc.

## Exorcism of Water.

> I exorcise you, creature of Water, in the name of God the Father almighty †, and in the name of Jesus Christ † his Son, our Lord, and in the power of the Holy † Spirit; that you may become exorcised water to put to flight all power of the enemy, and that you may be able to root out and displace the enemy himself with his apostate angels. By the power of the same our Lord Jesus Christ, who is to come to judge the living, and the dead, and the **world by fire** <note>original: "seculum per ignem"—a traditional liturgical formula referencing the final judgment</note>. Amen.

### Prayer.

> O God, who for the salvation of the human race established the greatest sacraments in the substance of waters; be favorably present to our invocations, and into this element prepared by manifold purifications, pour the power of your blessing, so that your **creature** <term>creatura: in a liturgical sense, this refers to a created element of the natural world (water, salt, oil) being addressed before it is consecrated for holy use</term>, serving your mysteries, may receive the effect of divine grace for driving away demons and dispelling diseases. Let whatever this wave sprinkles in the houses or places of the faithful be free from all uncleanness and delivered from harm; let no pestilential spirit reside there, nor any corrupting air; let all the snares of the lurking <unclear>enemy</unclear> depart, and <unclear>if</unclear> there is anything that envies either the safety or the peace of those dwelling there, let it flee at the sprinkling of this water, so that the wholesomeness sought through the invocation of your holy name may be defended from all attacks.

### Concerning things pertaining to the expulsion of the Devil, by visible means.
### Chapter 16.

<margin>The Devil is tortured by visible things.</margin>Experience testifies to how bitterly the evil spirits who reside in human bodies are tortured by the application of suitable visible objects. For by this means, the fervor of the Exorcist is stirred to persist more effectively, and the faith, hope, and devotion of the obsessed person are increased to resist more strongly. And as these things appear to be signs of the victory soon to be won (as the wicked spirits fear), they cannot but bear it most painfully. Therefore, the Exorcist ought to understand these kinds of visible things and the manner of their use. Let him know, therefore, that such things fall into two distinct categories: some are effective for treating the demon with **contempt** <note>In the context of exorcism, "contempt" (contemptus) refers to the practice of shaming or mocking the demon to break its pride and weaken its hold on the victim</note>, and others for suppressing him. There are two things that pertain to contempt: the first is the writing of the Demon’s name; the second is the painting of an image. 

<margin>On how the Devil is to be treated with contempt.</margin>For regarding the name of the principal spirit, or of the leaders (if there are many), an interrogation of the evil spirits must first be made, as stated above in Chapter 10. This will be known either by their own revelation or not. If the former happens, let the name be written on a sheet of parchment in large and clear letters. But if the latter, mocking names shall be invented (as some prefer), or rather insulting ones, as said above in Chapter 11; and let them be written similarly, in order to proceed against him in the manner to be described below. The second thing pertaining to contempt is that a representative image of the principal spirit occupying the human body should be depicted on the aforementioned paper, in a horrific and foul likeness. 

<margin>An image of the Demon is to be depicted, and the name written.</margin>These are the things that pertain to contempt, which perhaps will be more conveniently arranged by an experienced exorcist in this way: namely, that once the Demon has been depicted, there should be written above it on the same paper...

## Concerning smoldering substances for the expulsion of Evil Spells and Diabolical Incursions.
### Chapter XVII.

<margin>Devils are driven out by smoke.</margin>
The sacred scriptures testify (in the Book of Tobit, chapter 6) that every kind of demon is uprooted from either a man or a woman by the smoke of the burned pieces of the heart of a great fish, which was caught by Tobias at the command of an Angel. <note>In the biblical Book of Tobit, the Archangel Raphael instructs young Tobias to burn the heart and liver of a fish to drive away a demon named Asmodeus.</note> Furthermore, the teachings of certain Philosophers prove that the same result can be achieved by the smoke of various simple plants and composite mixtures. Therefore, I do not think I labor in vain by speaking here of fragrant fumigations. I propose that there are two types of odorous substances: the first of which is directly opposed to malignant spirits; the second is a destroyer of <term>Maleficia</term> <note>original: "Maleficiorum." In this context, it refers specifically to harmful magic, curses, or "witchcraft" rather than general evil.</note>. The first is as follows:

<margin>Fumigation against malignant spirits.</margin>
Take: 
Seeds of St. John’s Wort <note>original: "Hypericonis." Historically known as "fuga daemonum" or "devil's flight" because of its reputed power to ward off evil.</note>,
Rue <note>original: "Rutæ." A bitter herb often called the "Herb of Grace."</note>, and
Frankincense. } 6 Drachms <note>A drachm is an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams.</note> of each.

<margin>Fumigation against evil spells.</margin>
The second, however, ought to be like this:
Take:
The finest Frankincense,
Myrrh,
Storax Calamita <note>A fragrant resin used since antiquity in incense and medicine.</note>,
Galbanum <note>A bitter, aromatic gum resin derived from certain Persian plant species.</note>,
Ladanum <note>A sticky resin obtained from Cistus (rockrose) shrubs.</note>, and
Cloves. } 10 Drachms of each.
Two grains of Musk.

And crush all these together, but not too finely.
Having proposed these, it should be noted that even if the first mixture is directly opposed to malignant spirits, it is also effective for the second purpose; just as if the second directly opposes evil spells, it is nonetheless also effective for the first;

Through our Lord Jesus Christ, your Son...

### ->Concerning things pertaining to the overthrow of the Devil and of Spells, to be applied through Taste. Chapter XVIII.<-

Among the number of sensory things used to suppress the Demon and destroy spells, those pertaining to taste seem to be primary. Of these, there are two principal types: the first pertains to nourishment and directly opposes the Devil; the second pertains to medicine and primarily opposes <term>Maleficium</term> <note>original: "Maleficio." Here referring to harmful magic or a "curse" that has physically affected the victim's body.</note>. The first type is twofold: one is edible; the other is drinkable. That which is edible <margin>Goat's meat greatly provokes Demons.</margin> is also twofold: the first is bread; the second is goat's meat, of which it is said that it greatly provokes Demons. Whence, if for thirty continuous days a person believed to be vexed by a Demon is unable to eat it, it is judged

# P R E F A C E.

...possessed by a Demon? Chapter 11, Verse 11, Question 11 in the Text and in the gloss <note>original: "glo." Drinkable things are also of two types: the first is wine; the second is water, which is used for drinking by those who also suffer from a fever and, by a doctor's order, either abstain entirely from wine or fast from it in part. However, edible things, just like drinkable things, should not be consumed unless they are first affected by the following blessing. An exorcism of nutrients to be consumed.

<margin>Exorcism of edibles and drinkables against evil spirits.</margin>
I exorcise you, present preparation of nourishment, by the Lord God Almighty, three and one, who rained down bread from heaven for his people of Israel to eat in the wilderness; by God, who commanded the flesh of the sacrifice of thanksgiving to be eaten on that same day of immolation; by God, who in the sanctification of the first-fruits of the Harvest, willed not only a yearling Lamb to be slaughtered as a burnt offering, and other such things, but also a libation of wine of a fourth part of a <term>Hin</term> <note>A 'Hin' was an ancient Hebrew unit of liquid measure, approximately 1.5 gallons or 5.7 liters.</note> to be offered; and finally by God, whose spirit, in the creation of heaven and earth, was carried over the waters, afterwards divided by the firmament; that you may be to all who consume you, health of mind and body; a driving away of visible and invisible enemies; and the uprooting of all <term>maleficia</term> <note>original: "maleficiorum" - refers here to harmful magic, spells, or curses believed to be physically present in the body.</note>; may you be a safeguard of health, a strong shield, an impregnable wall to divinely repel every power rebelling against divine power: Through the same God, and our Lord Jesus Christ, who will come to judge the living and the dead, and the world by fire. Amen.

->Prayer.<-

...kindly grant, that while the impious adversary of your divine majesty is proud, and boasts of dwelling in human bodies, he may be cheated of his desire, and be caught in the schemes by which he thinks to do harm for a long time—either by his personal indwelling, or by a harmful infestation—with those who are about to consume the present edibles and drinkables being disturbed by his onset. The second thing pertaining to taste, which immediately opposes <term>Maleficium</term> <note>original: "Maleficio." This refers to "harmful magic" or a "witch's curse," specifically one that has been physically ingested.</note>, is called a medicine. And it is threefold: The first is called *preceding*, and pertains to the dissolving of the Witchcraft; the second is called *consequent*, and pertains to the expulsion or vomiting of the same; the third is called *subsequent*, and pertains to the strengthening of the stomach exhausted by vomiting. The first is as follows:

<margin>Medicine against witchcraft.</margin>
Take: Half a <term>drachm</term> <note>An apothecary weight equal to 1/8 of an ounce, or roughly 3.9 grams.</note> of the seeds of **Herb Paris** <note>Also known as "True Lover's Knot," this plant was historically used in folk medicine to treat madness or as an antidote to poisons.</note>.
A sufficient amount of a decoction of **Borage** <note>A common Mediterranean herb often associated with lifting the spirits and treating melancholy.</note>;
Mix, and let a potion be made, to be taken in the morning, repeated at various times according to the discretion of the **Exorcist**. The second is that which follows:

Take: One pound of Broth.
Three ounces of simple **Oxymel** <note>A medicinal syrup made of honey and vinegar, used since the time of Hippocrates to aid digestion and clear phlegm.</note>.
A little vinegar.

<margin>Medicine to vomit up the matter of the witchcraft.</margin>
Mix, and let a potion be made for a **Vomitory** <note>An emetic or substance used to induce vomiting, believed to physically expel the "material" or object of a curse from the body.</note>. This being established, it should be noted that if it happens that the present Vomitory does not work according to the intention, let the following be administered, as it is of a more effective nature:

Take: Three ounces of **Radish** juice.
One ounce of common oil.

<margin>Medicine for strengthening the stomach.</margin>

Today the word survives in various cultures as a syrup, often made from almonds or rice.</note>, and let it be given to the patient, both for the sake of strengthening the stomach, and for its cleansing from the remains of the infection arising from the agitation of the vomiting.

⁕. Refer to:

### *Exorcism of the medicines to be consumed.*

<margin>*Exorcism of all medicines to be consumed against witchcraft.*</margin>

> I exorcise you, substance of this medicinal mixture, by the Lord God, the creator of your power. He is the one who, through His Holy Angel, commanded His servant Tobias to gut a fish and preserve its heart, gall, and liver, as they were necessary for useful medicines. May you retain your natural power and receive a supernatural power to restrain every strength of Diabolical fraud and assault. This occurs not by our pleading merits, but by the power of the same God meeting us, and through the intercession of the passion of His Son, who is to come to judge the living and the dead, and the world by fire. Amen. <note>This refers to the Book of Tobit, where the Archangel Raphael instructs Tobias on how to use fish organs to drive away a demon and heal blindness. It serves as a biblical precedent for using physical substances in exorcism.</note>

### *Prayer.*

> O God, from whom all healing is to be expected from heaven, just as all creation of medicine is from the earth; you who in the fullness of time sent the Great Physician from heaven because you knew the great invalid lay upon the earth: multiply your mercy upon us, and deign to bless † and sanctify † this kind of medicinal compound. May whoever takes from it be freed from the harassment of the Devil—especially that brought about by <term>witchcraft</term> <note>original: "maleficio."</note>—and may its bonds be so effectively dissolved that, recognizing our Lord Jesus Christ was present, we may rejoice; He who lives and reigns with you in the unity of the Holy Spirit, God, for ever and ever. Amen.

Thus, therefore, with these preparations pertaining to the sense of taste having been prepared as piously as they were diligently, let the Exorcist strive to oppose the Devil and his harmful infestations.
